[tool-1a6] read_file ->      1	= Hacking the compiler :camel:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      2	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      3	This document is a work-in-progress attempt to provide useful
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      4	information for people willing to inspect or modify the compiler
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      5	distribution's codebase. Feel free to improve it by sending change
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      6	proposals for it.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      7	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      8	If you already have a patch that you would like to contribute to the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->      9	official distribution, please see link:CONTRIBUTING.md[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     10	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     11	=== Your first compiler modification
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     12	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     13	1. Create a new git branch to store your changes.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     14	+
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     15	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     16	git checkout -b my-modification
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     17	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     18	Usually, this branch wants to be based on `trunk`. If your changes must be on a
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     19	specific release, use its release branch (*not* the release tag) instead. For
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     20	example, to make a fix for 4.11.1, base your branch on *4.11* (not on *4.11.1*).
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     21	The `configure` step for the compiler recognises a development build from the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     22	`+dev` in the version number (see file `VERSION`), and release tarballs and the tagged Git commits do
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     23	not have this which causes some important development things to be disabled
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     24	(ocamltest and converting C compiler warnings to errors).
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     25	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     26	2. Consult link:INSTALL.adoc[] for build instructions. Here is the gist of it:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     27	+
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     28	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     29	./configure
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     30	make -j 4
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     31	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     32	If you are on a release build and need development options, you can add
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     33	`--enable-ocamltest` (to allow running the testsuite) and `--enable-warn-error`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     34	(so you don't get caught by CI later!).
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     35	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     36	3. Try the newly built compiler binaries `ocamlc`, `ocamlopt` or their
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     37	`.opt` version. To try the toplevel, use:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     38	+
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     39	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     40	make runtop
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     41	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     42	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     43	4. Hack frenetically and keep rebuilding.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     44	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     45	5. Run the testsuite from time to time.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     46	+
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     47	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     48	make tests
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     49	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     50	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     51	6. You did it, Well done! Consult link:CONTRIBUTING.md[] to send your contribution upstream.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     52	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     53	See also our <<tips,development tips and tricks>>, for example on how to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     54	<<opam-switch,create an opam switch>> to test your modified compiler.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     55	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     56	=== What to do
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     57	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     58	There is always a lot of potential tasks, both for old and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     59	newcomers. Here are various potential projects:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     60	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     61	* https://github.com/ocaml/ocaml/issues[The OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     62	  bugtracker] contains reported bugs and feature requests. Some
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     63	  changes that should be accessible to newcomers are marked with the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     64	  tag link:++https://github.com/ocaml/ocaml/issues?q=is%3Aopen+is%3Aissue+label%3Anewcomer-job++[
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     65	  newcomer-job].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     66	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     67	* The
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     68	  https://github.com/ocamllabs/compiler-hacking/wiki/Things-to-work-on[OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     69	  Labs compiler-hacking wiki] contains various ideas of changes to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     70	  propose, some easy, some requiring a fair amount of work.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     71	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     72	* Documentation improvements are always much appreciated, either in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     73	  the various `.mli` files or in the official manual
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     74	  (See link:manual/README.md[]). If you invest effort in understanding
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     75	  a part of the codebase, submitting a pull request that adds
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     76	  clarifying comments can be an excellent contribution to help you,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     77	  next time, and other code readers.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     78	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     79	* The https://github.com/ocaml/ocaml[github project] contains a lot of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     80	  pull requests, many of them being in dire need of a review -- we
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     81	  have more people willing to contribute changes than to review
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     82	  someone else's change. Picking one of them, trying to understand the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     83	  code (looking at the code around it) and asking questions about what
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     84	  you don't understand or what feels odd is super-useful. It helps the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     85	  contribution process, and it is also an excellent way to get to know
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     86	  various parts of the compiler from the angle of a specific aspect or
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     87	  feature.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     88	+
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     89	Again, reviewing small or medium-sized pull requests is accessible to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     90	anyone with OCaml programming experience, and helps maintainers and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     91	other contributors. If you also submit pull requests yourself, a good
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     92	discipline is to review at least as many pull requests as you submit.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     93	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     94	== Structure of the compiler
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     95	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     96	The compiler codebase can be intimidating at first sight. Here are
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     97	a few pointers to get started.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     98	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->     99	=== Compilation pipeline
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    100	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    101	==== The driver -- link:driver/[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    102	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    103	The driver contains the "main" function of the compilers that drive
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    104	compilation. It parses the command-line arguments and composes the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    105	required compiler passes by calling functions from the various parts
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    106	of the compiler described below.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    107	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    108	==== Parsing -- link:parsing/[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    109	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    110	Parses source files and produces an Abstract Syntax Tree (AST)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    111	(link:parsing/parsetree.mli[] has lot of helpful comments). See
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    112	link:parsing/HACKING.adoc[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    113	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    114	The logic for Camlp4 and Ppx preprocessing is not in link:parsing/[],
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    115	but in link:driver/[], see link:driver/pparse.mli[] and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    116	link:driver/pparse.ml[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    117	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    118	==== Typing -- link:typing/[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    119	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    120	Type-checks the AST and produces a typed representation of the program
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    121	(link:typing/typedtree.mli[] has some helpful comments). See
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    122	link:typing/HACKING.adoc[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    123	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    124	==== The bytecode compiler -- link:bytecomp/[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    125	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    126	==== The native compiler -- link:middle_end/[] and link:asmcomp/[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    127	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    128	=== Runtime system
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    129	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    130	The low-level routines that OCaml programs use during their execution:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    131	garbage collection, interaction with the operating system
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    132	(IO in particular), low-level primitives to manipulate some OCaml data
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    133	structures, etc. Mostly implemented in C, with some rare bits of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    134	assembly code in architecture-specific files. The "includes"
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    135	corresponding to the `.c` files are in the link:runtime/caml[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    136	subdirectory.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    137	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    138	Some files are only used by bytecode programs, some only used by
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    139	native-compiled programs, but most of the runtime code is
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    140	common. (See `runtime_COMMON_C_SOURCES`, `runtime_BYTECODE_ONLY_C_SOURCES`,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    141	and `runtime_NATIVE_ONLY_C_SOURCES` in link:Makefile[] for the list of common,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    142	bytecode-only, and native-only source files.)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    143	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    144	See link:runtime/HACKING.adoc[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    145	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    146	=== Libraries
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    147	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    148	link:stdlib/[]:: The standard library. Each file is largely
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    149	independent and should not need further knowledge.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    150	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    151	link:otherlibs/[]:: External libraries such as `unix`, `threads`,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    152	`dynlink` and `str`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    153	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    154	Instructions for building the full reference manual are provided in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    155	link:manual/README.md[]. However, if you only modify the documentation
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    156	comments in `.mli` files in the compiler codebase, you can observe the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    157	result by running
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    158	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    159	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    160	make html_doc
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    161	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    162	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    163	and then opening link:./api_docgen/ocamldoc/build/html/libref/index.html[] in a web browser.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    164	The documentation is located in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    165	link:./api_docgen/odoc/build/html/libref/index.html[] when `--with-odoc` is
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    166	passed to the configure script.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    167	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    168	=== Tools
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    169	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    170	link:lex/[]:: The `ocamllex` lexer generator.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    171	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    172	link:yacc/[]:: The `ocamlyacc` parser generator. We do not recommend
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    173	using it for user projects in need of a parser generator. Please
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    174	consider using and contributing to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    175	link:http://gallium.inria.fr/~fpottier/menhir/[menhir] instead, which
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    176	has tons of extra features, lets you write more readable grammars, and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    177	has excellent documentation.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    178

[tool-1a6] read_file ->    215	  toplevel/::             interactive system
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    216	  typing/::               typechecking -- see link:typing/HACKING.adoc[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    217	  utils/::                utility libraries
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    218	  winpthreads/::          winpthreads submodule -- see <<winpthreads,further>>
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    219	  yacc/::                 parser generator
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    220	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    221	[#tips]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    222	== Development tips and tricks
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    223	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    224	=== Keep merge commits when merging and cherry-picking Github PRs
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    225	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    226	Having the Github PR number show up in the git log is very useful for
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    227	later triaging. We recently disabled the "Rebase and merge" button,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    228	precisely because it does not produce a merge commit.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    229	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    230	When you cherry-pick a PR in another branch, please cherry-pick this
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    231	merge-style commit rather than individual commits, whenever
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    232	possible. (Picking a merge commit typically requires the `-m 1`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    233	option.) You should also use the `-x` option to include the hash of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    234	the original commit in the commit message.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    235	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    236	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    237	git cherry-pick -x -m 1 <merge-commit-hash>
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    238	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    239	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    240	=== Code style
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    241	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    242	Keep the style of the code you’re modifying. We don’t enforce the use of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    243	automated formatters. For OCaml code,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    244	https://github.com/OCamlPro/ocp-indent[ocp-indent] has been used.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    245	We use https://editorconfig.org/[EditorConfig] for simple styling. Lots of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    246	editors support EditorConfig
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    247	https://editorconfig.org/#pre-installed[out-of-the-box], or with
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    248	https://editorconfig.org/#download[plugins].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    249	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    250	[#opam-switch]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    251	=== Testing with `opam`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    252	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    253	If you are working on a development version of the compiler, you can create an
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    254	opam switch from it by running the following from the development repository:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    255	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    256	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    257	opam switch create . --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    258	opam install .
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    259	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    260	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    261	If you want to test someone else's development version from a public
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    262	git repository, you can build a switch directly (without cloning their
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    263	work locally) by pinning:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    264	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    265	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    266	opam switch create my-switch-name --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    267	opam pin add ocaml-variants git+https://$REPO#branch
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    268	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    269	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    270	==== Incremental builds with `opam`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    271	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    272	This section documents some tips to speed up your workflow when you need to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    273	alternate between testing your branch and patching the compiler.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    274	We'll assume that you're currently in a clone of the compiler's source code.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    275	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    276	===== Initial setup
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    277	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    278	For the rest of the section to work, you'll need your compiler to be
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    279	configured in the same way as `opam` would have configured it. The simplest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    280	way is to run the normal commands for the switch initialization, with the extra
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    281	`--inplace-build` flag:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    282	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    283	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    284	opam switch create . --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    285	opam install . --inplace-build
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    286	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    287	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    288	However, if you need specific configuration options, you can also configure it
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    289	manually, as long as you make sure that the configuration prefix is the one
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    290	where `opam` would install the compiler.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    291	You will then need to install the compiler, either from the working directory
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    292	(that you must build yourself) or using the regular sandboxed builds.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    293	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    294	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    295	# Example with regular opam build
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    296	opam switch create . --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    297	opam install .
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    298	./configure --prefix=$(opam var prefix) # put extra configuration args here
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    299	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    300	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    301	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    302	# Example with installation from the current directory
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    303	opam switch create . --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    304	./configure --prefix=$(opam var prefix) # put extra configuration args here
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    305	make -j
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    306	opam install . --assume-built
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    307	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    308	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    309	===== Basic workflow
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    310	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    311	We will assume that the workflow alternates between work on the compiler and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    312	external (`opam`-related) commands.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    313	As an example, debugging an issue in the compiler can be done by a first step
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    314	that triggers the issue (by installing a given `opam` package), then adding
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    315	some logging to the compiler, re-trigger the issue, and based on the logs either
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    316	add more logging, or try a patch, and so on.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    317	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    318	The part of this workflow that we're going to optimize is when we switch from
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    319	working on the compiler to using the compiler. The basic way to do this is to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    320	run `opam install .` again, but this will recompile the compiler from scratch
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    321	and also trigger a recompilation of all the packages in the switch.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    322	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    323	===== Using `opam-custom-install`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    324	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    325	The `opam-custom-install` plugin allows you to install a package using a custom
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    326	command instead of the package-supplied one. It can be installed following
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    327	instructions https://gitlab.ocamlpro.com/louis/opam-custom-install[here].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    328	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    329	In our case, we need to build the compiler, and when we've built everything
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    330	that we need then we run `opam custom-install ocaml-variants -- make install`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    331	This will make `opam` remove the previously installed version of the compiler
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    332	(if any), then install the new one in its stead.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    333	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    334	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    335	# reinstall the compiler, and rebuild all opam packages
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    336	opam custom-install ocaml-variants -- make install
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    337	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    338	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    339	Since most `opam` packages depend on the compiler, this will trigger a
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    340	reinstallation of all the packages in the switch.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    341	If you want to avoid that (for instance, your patch only adds some logging
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    342	so you expect the core libraries and all the already compiled packages to be
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    343	identical), you can use the additional `--no-recompilations` flag.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    344	There are no checks that it's safe to do so, so if your patch ends up
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    345	changing even slightly one of the core libraries' files, you will likely
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    346	get inconsistent assumptions errors later.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    347	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    348	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    349	# reinstall the compiler, leaving the opam packages untouched -- unsafe!
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    350	opam custom-install --no-recompilations ocaml-variants -- make install
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    351	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    352	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    353	Note about the first installation:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    354	When you start from an empty switch, and install a compiler (in our case,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    355	the `ocaml-variants` package provided by the compiler's `opam` file), then
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    356	a number of additional packages are installed to ensure that the switch
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    357	will work correctly. Mainly, the `ocaml` package needs to be installed,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    358	and while it's done automatically when using regular `opam` commands, the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    359	`custom-install` plugin will not force installation of dependencies.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    360	Moreover, if you try to fix the problem by manually installing the `ocaml`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    361	package, `opam` will try to recompile `ocaml-variants`, using the default
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    362	instructions. You can get around this by running
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    363	`opam reinstall --forget-pending` just after the `opam custom-install` command
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    364	and just before the `opam install ocaml command`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    365	Full example:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    366	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    367	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    368	opam switch create . --empty
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    369	./configure --prefix=$(opam var prefix) --disable-ocamldoc --disable-ocamltest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    370	make world && make opt
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    371	opam custom-install ocaml-variants -- make install
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    372	opam reinstall --forget-pending --yes
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    373	opam install ocaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    374	# You now have a working switch, in which you can start installing packages
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    375	-----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    376	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    377	One advantage of this plugin over a plain `make install` is that it
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    378	correctly tracks the files associated with the compiler, so if your
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    379	`make install` command only installs the bytecode versions of the tools,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    380	then with `opam-custom-install` you will end up in a state where only the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    381	bytecode tools are installed, whereas with a raw `make install` you will have
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    382	stale native binaries remaining in your switch.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    383	Since it's significantly faster to build the bytecode version of the tools,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    384	and many `opam` packages will pick the native version of the compilers if
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    385	present and the bytecode version otherwise, you can build your initial switch
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    386	with the native versions (to get quickly to a state where a bug appears),
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    387	then clean your working directory and start building bytecode tools only
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    388	for the actual debugging phase.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    389	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    390	===== Without `opam-custom-install`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    391	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    392	You can achieve some improvements using built-in `opam` commands.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    393	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    394	Using `opam install . --assume-built` will simply remove the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    395	package for the compiler, then run the installation instructions
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    396	(`make install`) in the working directory, tracking the installed files
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    397	correctly. The main difference with the `opam-custom-install` version
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    398	is that there's no way to prevent this command from triggering a full
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    399	recompilation of your switch.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    400	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    401	You can also run `make install` manually, which will not trigger a
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    402	recompilation, but will not remove the previous version either and can
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    403	mess with `opam`'s tracking of installed files.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    404	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    405	=== Useful Makefile targets and options
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    406	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    407	Besides the targets listed in link:INSTALL.adoc[] for build and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    408	installation, the following targets may be of use:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    409	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    410	`make runtop` :: builds and runs the ocaml toplevel of the distribution
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    411	                          (optionally uses `rlwrap` for readline+history support)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    412	                          (use `make runtop-with-otherlibs` if you need `Unix` or other
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    413	                           `otherlibs/` libraries)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    414	`make natruntop`:: builds and runs the native ocaml toplevel (experimental)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    415	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    416	`make partialclean`:: Clean the OCaml files but keep the compiled C files.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    417	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    418	`make depend`:: Regenerate the `.depend` file. Should be used each time new dependencies are added between files.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    419	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    420	`make -C testsuite parallel`:: see link:testsuite/HACKING.adoc[]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    421	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    422	You can use `make foo V=1` to build the target foo and show full
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    423	commands instead of abbreviated names like OCAMLC, etc. This can be
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    424	useful to know the flags to use to manually rebuild a file.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    425	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    426	Additionally, there are some developer specific targets in link:Makefile.dev[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    427	These targets are automatically available when working in a Git clone of the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    428	repository, but are not available from a tarball.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    429	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    430	=== Automatic configure options
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    431	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    432	If you have options to `configure` which you always (or at least frequently)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    433	use, it's possible to store them in Git, and `configure` will automatically add
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    434	them. For example, you may wish to avoid building the debug runtime by default
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    435	while developing, in which case you can issue
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    436	`git config --global ocaml.configure '--disable-debug-runtime'`. The `configure`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    437	script will alert you that it has picked up this option and added it _before_
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    438	any options you specified for `configure`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    439	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    440	Options are added before those passed on the command line, so it's possible to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    441	override them, for example `./configure --enable-debug-runtime` will build the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    442	debug runtime, since the enable flag appears after the disable flag. You can
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    443	also use the full power of Git's `config` command and have options specific to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    444	particular clone or worktree.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    445	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    446	=== Speeding up configure
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    447	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    448	`configure` includes the standard `-C` option which caches various test results
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    449	in the file `config.cache` and can use those results to avoid running tests in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    450	subsequent invocations. This mechanism works fine, except that it is easy to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    451	clean the cache by mistake (e.g. with `git clean -dfX`). The cache is also
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    452	host-specific which means the file has to be deleted if you run `configure` with
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    453	a new `--host` value (this is quite common on Windows, where `configure` is
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    454	also quite slow to run).
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    455	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    456	You can elect to have host-specific cache files by issuing
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    457	`git config --global ocaml.configure-cache .`. The `configure` script will now
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    458	automatically create `ocaml-host.cache` (e.g. `ocaml-x86_64-pc-windows.cache`,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    459	or `ocaml-default.cache`). If you work with multiple worktrees, you can share
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    460	these cache files by issuing `git config --global ocaml.configure-cache ..`. The
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    461	directory is interpreted _relative_ to the `configure` script.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    462	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    463	=== Bootstrapping
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    464	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    465	The OCaml compiler is bootstrapped. This means that
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    466	previously-compiled bytecode versions of the compiler and lexer are
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    467	included in the repository under the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    468	link:boot/[] directory. These bytecode images are used once the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    469	bytecode runtime (which is written in C) has been built to compile the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    470	standard library and then to build a fresh compiler. Details can be
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    471	found in link:BOOTSTRAP.adoc[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    472	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    473	=== Speeding up builds
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    474	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    475	Once you've built a natively-compiled `ocamlc.opt`, you can use it to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    476	speed up future builds by copying it to `boot`:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    477	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    478	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    479	cp ocamlc.opt boot/
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    480	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    481	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    482	If `boot/ocamlc` changes (e.g. because you ran `make bootstrap`), then
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    483	the build will revert to the slower bytecode-compiled `ocamlc` until
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    484	you do the above step again.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    485	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    486	=== Using merlin
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    487	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    488	During the development of the compiler, the internal format of compiled object
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    489	files evolves, and quickly becomes incompatible with the format of the last
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    490	OCaml release. In particular, even an up-to-date merlin will be unable to use
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    491	them during most of the development cycle: opening a compiler source file with
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    492	merlin gives a frustrating error message.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    493	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    494	To use merlin on the compiler, you want to build the compiler with an older
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    495	version of itself. One easy way to do this is to use the experimental build
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    496	rules for Dune, which are distributed with the compiler (with no guarantees that
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    497	the build will work all the time). Assuming you already have a recent OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    498	version installed with merlin and dune, you can just run the following from the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    499	compiler sources:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    500	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    501	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    502	./configure # if not already done
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    503	make clean && dune build @libs
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    504	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    505	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    506	which will do a bytecode build of all the distribution (without linking
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    507	the executables), using your OCaml compiler.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    508	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    509	Merlin will be looking at the artefacts generated by dune (in `_build`), rather
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    510	than trying to open the incompatible artefacts produced by a Makefile build. In
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    511	particular, you need to repeat the dune build every time you change the interface
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    512	of some compilation unit, so that merlin is aware of the new interface.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    513	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    514	You only need to run `configure` once, but you will need to run `make clean`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    515	every time you want to run `dune` after you built something with `make`;
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    516	otherwise dune will complain that build artefacts are present among the sources.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    517	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    518	Finally, there will be times where the compiler simply cannot be built with an
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    519	older version of itself. One example of this is when a new primitive is added to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    520	the runtime, and then used in the standard library straight away, since the rest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    521	of the compiler requires the `stdlib` library to build, nothing can be build. In
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    522	such situations, you will have to either live without merlin, or develop on an
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    523	older branch of the compiler, for example the maintenance branch of the last
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    524	released version. Developing a patch from a release branch can later introduce a
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    525	substantial amount of extra work, when you rebase to the current development
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    526	version. But it also makes it a lot easier to test the impact of your work on
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    527	third-party code, by installing a local <<opam-switch,opam switch>>: opam
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    528	packages tend to be compatible with released versions of the compiler, whereas
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    529	most packages are incompatible with the in-progress development version.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    530	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    531	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    532	=== License
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    533	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    534	The OCaml distribution in this repository, which includes the OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    535	compiler and runtime, the OCaml standard library, and some extra
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    536	tools, is licensed under the LGPL version 2.1, with a linking
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    537	exception. See link:LICENSE[] for the full license text. Using the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    538	https://spdx.org/licenses/[SPDX] standard for license identifiers,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    539	this is the license "LGPL-2.1 WITH
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    540	https://spdx.org/licenses/OCaml-LGPL-linking-exception.html[OCaml-LGPL-linking-exception]".
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    541	Let us include here a few explanations about the intent of this
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    542	licensing choice -- note that only the license text has legal value.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    543	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    544	The broad intent of the LGPL is that if you modify the OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    545	distribution, and distribute your modified version to your users, then
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    546	you have to provide the source code of your version -- the LGPL, like
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    547	the GPL and unlike the BSD and MIT licenses, is "hereditary"; modified
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    548	versions of LGPL programs must remain free software under the same
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    549	license. (For more general discussion of the LGPL license, see the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    550	https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-faq.en.html[FAQ] maintained by the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    551	GNU project.)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    552	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    553	On the other hand, if your code is not derived from the OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    554	distribution, it only uses it, then you can use the licensing terms of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    555	your choice. We use an explicit "linking exception" precisely to make
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    556	it clear that it is fine to link statically or dynamically with parts
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    557	of the OCaml distribution, typically the OCaml runtime and the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    558	standard library. Linking exceptions are a common practice for
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    559	language compilers and runtime libraries, for example the GCC project
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    560	uses such an exception. See the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    561	https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPL_linking_exception[Wikipedia article]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    562	on linking exceptions for more details.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    563	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    564	Finally: If you have your own fork of the OCaml distribution, it must
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    565	be distributed to your own users under the same license
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    566	(LGPL + exception), and your users will also benefit from the linking
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    567	exception.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    568	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    569	=== Continuous integration
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    570	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    571	[#check-typo]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    572	==== check-typo
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    573	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    574	The `tools/check-typo` script enforces various typographical rules in the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    575	OCaml compiler codebase.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    576	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    577	Running `./tools/check-typo` from the repository root will check all
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    578	source files. This can be fairly slow (2 minutes for example). Use
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    579	`./tools/check-typo <path>` to run it on some file or directory
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    580	(recursively) only.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    581	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    582	Running `./tools/check-typo-since trunk` checks all files that changed
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    583	in the commits since `trunk` -- this work with any git reference. It
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    584	runs much faster than a full `./tools/check-typo`, typically instantly.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    585	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    586	You can also setup a git commit-hook to automatically run `check-typo`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    587	on the changes you commit, by copying the file
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    588	`tools/pre-commit-githook` to `.git/hooks/pre-commit`. If changes in a
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    589	commit alter the `configure` script, the hook also checks that the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    590	committed `configure` script is up-to-date.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    591	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    592	Some files need special rules to opt out of `check-typo` checks; this
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    593	is specified in the `.gitattributes` file at the root of the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    594	repository, using `typo.foo` attributes.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    595	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    596	===== Regenerating the `configure` script
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    597	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    598	The `configure` script may be regenerated by running `tools/autogen`,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    599	with GNU Autoconf installed.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    600	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    601	If you're using a version of GNU Autoconf that differs from our CI,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    602	then the generated `configure` script might differ too. The latest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    603	Ubuntu LTS packages may be used as a reasonable base to generate this
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    604	file. Using a Docker image with GNU Autoconf installed, and `git
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    605	rebase --exec`, one can properly regenerate the `configure` script for
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    606	each commit in a branch.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    607	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    608	[source,sh]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    609	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    610	docker build -t ocaml-make-configure:latest - <<'EOF'
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    611	# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    612	FROM ubuntu:latest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    613	RUN rm -f /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/docker-clean; echo 'Binary::apt::APT::Keep-Downloaded-Packages "true";' > /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/keep-cache
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    614	RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/var/cache/apt,sharing=locked \
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    615	    --mount=type=cache,target=/var/lib/apt,sharing=locked \
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    616	    apt update && apt-get --no-install-recommends install -y autoconf make
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    617	WORKDIR /mnt
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    618	ENTRYPOINT ["tools/autogen"]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    619	EOF
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    620	git rebase trunk --exec "docker run --rm -it -v $PWD:/mnt ocaml-make-configure:latest"
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    621	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    622	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    623	==== GitHub's Continuous Integration: GitHub Actions and AppVeyor
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    624	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    625	The scripts that are run on GitHub Actions are described in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    626	link:.github/workflows/build.yml[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    627	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    628	For example, if you want to reproduce the default build on your
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    629	machine, you can use the configuration values and run command taken from
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    630	link:tools/ci/actions/runner.sh[]:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    631	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    632	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    633	bash -ex tools/ci/actions/runner.sh configure
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    634	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    635	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    636	The link:.github/workflows/hygiene.yml[] script supports other kinds of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    637	tests which inspect the patch submitted as part of a pull request. These
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    638	tests rely on ancillary data generated by GitHub Actions which you have to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    639	set explicitly to reproduce them locally.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    640	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    641	`Changes updated` checks that the link:Changes[] file has been modified
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    642	(hopefully to add a new entry). It can be disabled by including "_(no change
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    643	entry needed)_" in one of your commit messages -- but in general all patches
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    644	submitted should come with a Changes entry; see the guidelines in
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    645	link:CONTRIBUTING.md[].
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    646	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    647	The Windows ports take a long time to test - INRIA's precheck service is the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    648	best to use when all 6 Windows ports need testing for a branch, but the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    649	AppVeyor scripts also support the other ports. The matrix is controlled by
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    650	the following environment variables, which should be set in link:appveyor.yml[]:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    651	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    652	- `PORT` - this must be set on each job. Either `mingw`, `msvc` or `cygwin`
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    653	  followed by `32` or `64`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    654	- `BOOTSTRAP_FLEXDLL` - must be set on each job. Either `true` or `false`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    655	  At present, must be `false` for Cygwin builds. Controls whether flexlink
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    656	  is bootstrapped as part of the test or installed from a binary archive.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    657	- `FORCE_CYGWIN_UPGRADE`. Default: `0`. Set to `1` to force an upgrade of
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    658	  Cygwin packages as part of the build. Normally a full upgrade is only
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    659	  triggered if the packages installed require it.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    660	- `BUILD_MODE`. Default: `world.opt`. Either `world.opt`, `steps`, or `C`.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    661	  Controls whether the build uses the `world.opt` target or the classic
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    662	  `world`, `opt`, `opt.opt` targets. The `C` build is a fast test used to
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    663	  build just enough of the tree to cover the C sources (it's used to test
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    664	  old MSVC compilers).
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    665	- `SDK`. Defaults to Visual Studio 2022. Specifies the exact command to run
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    666	  to set-up the Microsoft build environment.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    667	- `CYGWIN_DIST`. Default: `64`. Either `64` or `32`, selects 32-bit or 64-bit
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    668	  Cygwin as the build environment.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    669	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    670	==== GitHub Actions Continuous Integration (CI)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    671	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    672	We use GitHub Actions workflows to test the OCaml compiler and runtime
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    673	in various settings. Some tests are not run by default and are
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    674	triggered by setting a label on the pull request. See below:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    675	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    676	- `CI: Full matrix`: expands the test matrix considerably with additional
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    677	  configurations; useful for PRs which affect the "systems" side of the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    678	  compiler;
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    679	- `CI: Skip testsuite`: turns the tesuite *off* for a PR, which can be useful
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    680	  for PRs which do not affect the compiler _at all_ or while a specific failure
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    681	  is being investigated;
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    682	- `run-crosscompiler-tests`: build various cross-compilers and test them;
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    683	- `run-thread-sanitizer`: build the distribution with the Thread
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    684	  Sanitizer (TSAN), and run the whole testsuite instrumented with TSAN;
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    685	- `run-multicoretests`: run an extensive testsuite convering multicore
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    686	  features.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    687	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    688	==== INRIA's Continuous Integration (CI)
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    689	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    690	INRIA provides a Jenkins continuous integration service that OCaml
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    691	uses, see link:https://ci.inria.fr/ocaml/[]. It provides a wider
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    692	architecture support (MSVC and MinGW, a zsystems s390x machine, and
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    693	various MacOS versions) than the Travis/AppVeyor testing on github,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    694	but only runs on commits to the trunk or release branches, not on every
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    695	PR.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    696	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    697	You do not need to be an INRIA employee to open an account on this
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    698	jenkins service; anyone can create an account there to access build
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    699	logs and manually restart builds. If you
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    700	would like to do this but have trouble doing it, please email
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    701	ocaml-ci-admin@inria.fr.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    702	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    703	To be notified by email of build failures, you can subscribe to the
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    704	ocaml-ci-notifications@inria.fr mailing list by visiting
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    705	https://sympa.inria.fr/sympa/info/ocaml-ci-notifications[its web page.]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    706	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    707	==== Running INRIA's CI on a publicly available git branch
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    708	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    709	If you have suspicions that your changes may fail on exotic architectures
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    710	(they touch the build system or the backend code generator,
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    711	for example) and would like to get wider testing than github's CI
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    712	provides, it is possible to manually start INRIA's CI on arbitrary git
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    713	branches even before opening a pull request as follows:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    714	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    715	1. Make sure you have an account on Inria's CI as described before.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    716	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    717	2. Make sure you have been added to the ocaml project.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    718	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    719	3. Prepare a branch with the code you'd like to test, say "mybranch". It
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    720	is probably a good idea to make sure your branch is based on the latest
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    721	trunk.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    722	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    723	4. Make your branch publicly available. For instance, you can fork
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    724	OCaml's GitHub repository and then push "mybranch" to your fork.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    725	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    726	5. Visit https://ci.inria.fr/ocaml/job/precheck and log in. Click on
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    727	"Build with parameters".
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    728	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    729	6. Fill in the REPO_URL and BRANCH fields as appropriate and run the build.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    730	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    731	7. You should receive a bunch of e-mails with the build logs for each
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    732	slave and each tested configuration (with and without flambda) attached.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    733	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    734	==== Changing what the CI does
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    735	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    736	INRIA's CI "main" and "precheck" jobs run the script
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    737	tools/ci-build. In particular, when running the CI on a publicly
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    738	available branch via the "precheck" job as explained in the previous
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    739	section, you can edit this script to change what the CI will test.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    740	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    741	For instance, parallel builds are only tested for the "trunk"
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    742	branch. In order to use "precheck" to test parallel build on a custom
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    743	branch, add this at the beginning of tools/ci-build:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    744	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    745	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    746	OCAML_JOBS=10
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    747	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    748	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    749	=== The `caml-commits` mailing list
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    750	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    751	If you would like to receive email notifications of all commits made to the main
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    752	git repository, you can subscribe to the caml-commits@inria.fr mailing list by
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    753	visiting https://sympa.inria.fr/sympa/info/caml-commits[its web page.]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    754	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    755	[#winpthreads]
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    756	=== The `winpthreads` library for the MSVC port
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    757	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    758	The `winpthreads` library is used to emulate `pthread` for the MSVC
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    759	port. Upstream bundles it along with all the MinGW libraries so our
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    760	`winpthreads` submodule points to `git subtree` repository rather than
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    761	upstream directly.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    762	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    763	To recreate the `winpthreads` repository from upstream, you can do:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    764	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    765	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    766	git clone -o upstream https://git.code.sf.net/p/mingw-w64/mingw-w64 winpthreads
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    767	cd winpthreads
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    768	git checkout upstream/master
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    769	git branch -D master
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    770	git subtree -P mingw-w64-libraries/winpthreads split -b master
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    771	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    772	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    773	As subtree splitting is deterministic, repeating these operations later will
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    774	allow to update `master`, for instance by:
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    775	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    776	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    777	git fetch upstream
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    778	git checkout upstream/master
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    779	git subtree -P mingw-w64-libraries/winpthreads split -b tmp
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    780	git checkout master
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    781	git merge --ff-only tmp
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    782	git branch -d tmp
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    783	----
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    784	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    785	and then go on updating the `winpthreads` submodule in the `ocaml` repository.
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    786	
[tool-1a6] read_file ->    787	Happy Hacking!

[tool-e33] read_file ->     52	   the cycle and _strictly decreases_ to 0.
[tool-e33] read_file ->     53	
[tool-e33] read_file ->     54	   Domains created in a given cycle will not have any sweep work in that cycle.
[tool-e33] read_file ->     55	   Sweep changes GARBAGE coloured objects in the domain's own pools to FREE
[tool-e33] read_file ->     56	   (not a distinct colour; object header is set to 0) and adds them to the free
[tool-e33] read_file ->     57	   list. No object will have the GARBAGE colour in the domain's own pools since
[tool-e33] read_file ->     58	   the domain starts with an empty pool with no objects and new objects are

[tool-e33] read_file ->    210	Caml_inline void prefetch_block(value v)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    211	{
[tool-e33] read_file ->    212	  /* Prefetch a block so that scanning it later avoids cache misses.
[tool-e33] read_file ->    213	     We will access at least the header, but we don't yet know how
[tool-e33] read_file ->    214	     many of the fields we will access - the block might be already
[tool-e33] read_file ->    215	     marked, not scannable, or very short. The compromise here is to
[tool-e33] read_file ->    216	     prefetch the header and the first few fields.
[tool-e33] read_file ->    217	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    218	     We issue two prefetches, with the second being a few words ahead
[tool-e33] read_file ->    219	     of the first. Most of the time, these will land in the same
[tool-e33] read_file ->    220	     cacheline, be coalesced by hardware, and so not cost any more
[tool-e33] read_file ->    221	     than a single prefetch. Two memory operations are issued only
[tool-e33] read_file ->    222	     when the two prefetches land in different cachelines.
[tool-e33] read_file ->    223	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    224	     In the case where the block is not already in cache, and yet is
[tool-e33] read_file ->    225	     already marked, not markable, or extremely short, then we waste
[tool-e33] read_file ->    226	     somewhere between 1/8-1/2 of a prefetch operation (in expectation,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    227	     depending on alignment, word size, and cache line size), which is
[tool-e33] read_file ->    228	     cheap enough to make this worthwhile. */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    229	  caml_prefetch((const void *)Hp_val(v));
[tool-e33] read_file ->    230	  caml_prefetch((const void *)&Field(v, 3));
[tool-e33] read_file ->    231	}

[tool-e33] read_file ->    310	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    311	static intnat ephe_mark (intnat budget, uintnat for_cycle,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    312	                         /* Forces ephemerons and their data to be alive */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    313	                         int force_alive)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    314	{
[tool-e33] read_file ->    315	  value v, data, key, f, todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    316	  value* prev_linkp;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    317	  header_t hd;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    318	  mlsize_t size, i;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    319	  caml_domain_state* domain_state = Caml_state;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    320	  int alive_data;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    321	  intnat marked = 0, trivial_data = 0, made_live = 0;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    322	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    323	  if (domain_state->ephe_info->cursor.cycle == for_cycle &&
[tool-e33] read_file ->    324	      !force_alive) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    325	    prev_linkp = domain_state->ephe_info->cursor.todop;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    326	    todo = *prev_linkp;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    327	  } else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    328	    todo = domain_state->ephe_info->todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    329	    prev_linkp = &domain_state->ephe_info->todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    330	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    331	  while (todo != 0 && budget > 0) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    332	    v = todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    333	    todo = Ephe_link(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    334	    CAMLassert (Tag_val(v) == Abstract_tag);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    335	    hd = Hd_val(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    336	    data = Ephe_data(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    337	    alive_data = 1;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    338	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    339	    if (force_alive)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    340	      caml_darken (domain_state, v, 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    341	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    342	    /* If ephemeron is unmarked, data is dead */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    343	    if (is_unmarked(v)) alive_data = 0;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    344	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    345	    size = Wosize_hd(hd);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    346	    for (i = CAML_EPHE_FIRST_KEY; alive_data && i < size; i++) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    347	      key = Ephe_key(v, i);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    348	    ephemeron_again:
[tool-e33] read_file ->    349	      if (key != caml_ephe_none && Is_block(key)) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    350	        if (Tag_val(key) == Forward_tag) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    351	          f = Forward_val(key);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    352	          if (Is_block(f)) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    353	            if (Tag_val(f) == Forward_tag || Tag_val(f) == Lazy_tag ||
[tool-e33] read_file ->    354	                Tag_val(f) == Forcing_tag || Tag_val(f) == Double_tag) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    355	              /* Do not short-circuit the pointer */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    356	            } else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    357	              Field(v, i) = key = f;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    358	              goto ephemeron_again;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    359	            }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    360	          }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    361	        }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    362	        else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    363	          if (Tag_val (key) == Infix_tag) key -= Infix_offset_val (key);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    364	          if (is_unmarked (key))
[tool-e33] read_file ->    365	            alive_data = 0;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    366	        }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    367	      }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    368	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    369	    budget -= Whsize_wosize(i);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    370	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    371	    bool keep;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    372	    if (data == caml_ephe_none || Is_long(data)) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    373	      /* Not yet known whether this ephemeron's keys/block will be marked,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    374	         but since the data is trivial nothing will happen if they are,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    375	         so remove it from the todo list */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    376	      trivial_data++;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    377	      keep = false;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    378	    } else if (force_alive || alive_data) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    379	      /* This ephemeron's keys & block are marked, so mark the data,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    380	         and remove it from the todo list */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    381	      caml_darken (domain_state, data, 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    382	      made_live++;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    383	      keep = false;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    384	    } else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    385	      /* Leave this ephemeron on the todo list */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    386	      keep = true;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    387	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    388	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    389	    if (keep) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    390	      prev_linkp = &Ephe_link(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    391	    } else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    392	      Ephe_link(v) = domain_state->ephe_info->live;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    393	      domain_state->ephe_info->live = v;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    394	      *prev_linkp = todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    395	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    396	    marked++;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    397	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    398	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    399	  caml_gc_log ("Mark Ephemeron: %s. Ephemeron cycle=%" CAML_PRIdNAT " "
[tool-e33] read_file ->    400	               "examined=%" CAML_PRIdNAT " trivial_data=%" CAML_PRIdNAT " "
[tool-e33] read_file ->    401	               "marked=%" CAML_PRIdNAT,
[tool-e33] read_file ->    402	               domain_state->ephe_info->cursor.cycle == for_cycle ?
[tool-e33] read_file ->    403	                 "Continued from cursor" : "Discarded cursor",
[tool-e33] read_file ->    404	               for_cycle, marked, trivial_data, made_live);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    405	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    406	  domain_state->ephe_info->cursor.cycle = for_cycle;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    407	  domain_state->ephe_info->cursor.todop = prev_linkp;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    408	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    409	  return budget;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    410	}
[tool-e33] read_file ->    411	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    412	static intnat ephe_sweep (caml_domain_state* domain_state, intnat budget)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    413	{
[tool-e33] read_file ->    414	  value v;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    415	  CAMLassert (caml_gc_phase == Phase_sweep_ephe);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    416	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    417	  while (domain_state->ephe_info->todo != 0 && budget > 0) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    418	    v = domain_state->ephe_info->todo;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    419	    domain_state->ephe_info->todo = Ephe_link(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    420	    CAMLassert (Tag_val(v) == Abstract_tag);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    421	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    422	    if (is_unmarked(v)) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    423	      /* The whole array is dead, drop this ephemeron */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    424	      budget -= 1;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    425	    } else {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    426	      caml_ephe_clean(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    427	      Ephe_link(v) = domain_state->ephe_info->live;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    428	      domain_state->ephe_info->live = v;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    429	      budget -= Whsize_val(v);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    430	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    431	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    432	  return budget;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    433	}
[tool-e33] read_file ->    434	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    435	/*******************************************************************************
[tool-e33] read_file ->    436	 * Orphaning and adoption
[tool-e33] read_file ->    437	 ******************************************************************************/
[tool-e33] read_file ->    438	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    439	/* These are biased data structures left over from terminating domains.
[tool-e33] read_file ->    440	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    441	   Synchronization:
[tool-e33] read_file ->    442	   - operations that mutate the structure
[tool-e33] read_file ->    443	     (adding new orphaned values or adopting orphans)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    444	     are protected from each other using [orphaned_lock];
[tool-e33] read_file ->    445	     this is simpler than using atomic lists, and not performance-sensitive
[tool-e33] read_file ->    446	   - the read-only function [no_orphaned_work()] uses atomic accesses
[tool-e33] read_file ->    447	     to avoid taking a lock (it is called more often)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    448	 */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    449	static struct {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    450	  value _Atomic ephe_list_live;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    451	  struct caml_final_info * _Atomic final_info;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    452	} orph_structs = {0, NULL};
[tool-e33] read_file ->    453	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    454	static caml_plat_mutex orphaned_lock = CAML_PLAT_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    455

[tool-e33] read_file ->    463	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    464	  return last;

[tool-e33] read_file ->    483	void caml_orphan_ephemerons (caml_domain_state* domain_state)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    484	{
[tool-e33] read_file ->    485	  struct caml_ephe_info* ephe_info = domain_state->ephe_info;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    486	  if (ephe_info->todo == 0 &&
[tool-e33] read_file ->    487	      ephe_info->live == 0 &&
[tool-e33] read_file ->    488	      ephe_info->must_sweep_ephe == 0)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    489	    return;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    490	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    491	  /* Force all ephemerons and their data on todo list to be alive */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    492	  if (ephe_info->todo) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    493	    while (ephe_info->todo) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    494	      ephe_mark (100000, 0, EPHE_MARK_FORCE_ALIVE);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    495	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    496	    ephe_todo_list_emptied ();
[tool-e33] read_file ->    497	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    498	  CAMLassert (ephe_info->todo == 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    499	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    500	  if (ephe_info->live) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    501	    value live_tail = ephe_list_tail(ephe_info->live);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    502	    CAMLassert(Ephe_link(live_tail) == 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    503	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    504	    caml_plat_lock_blocking(&orphaned_lock);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    505	    Ephe_link(live_tail) = orph_structs.ephe_list_live;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    506	    orph_structs.ephe_list_live = ephe_info->live;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    507	    ephe_info->live = 0;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    508	    caml_plat_unlock(&orphaned_lock);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    509	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    510	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    511	  if (ephe_info->must_sweep_ephe) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    512	    ephe_info->must_sweep_ephe = 0;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    513	    (void)caml_atomic_counter_decr(&num_domains_to_ephe_sweep);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    514	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    515	  CAMLassert (ephe_info->must_sweep_ephe == 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    516	  CAMLassert (ephe_info->live == 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    517	  CAMLassert (ephe_info->todo == 0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    518	}

[tool-e33] read_file ->    520	void caml_orphan_finalisers (caml_domain_state* domain_state)
[tool-e33] read_file ->    521	{
[tool-e33] read_file ->    522	  struct caml_final_info* f = domain_state->final_info;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    523	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    524	  if (f->todo_head != NULL || f->first.size != 0 || f->last.size != 0) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    525	    /* have some final structures */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    526	    (void)caml_atomic_counter_incr(&num_domains_orphaning_finalisers);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    527	    if (caml_gc_phase != Phase_sweep_and_mark_main) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    528	      /* Force a major GC cycle to simplify constraints for orphaning
[tool-e33] read_file ->    529	         finalisers. See note attached to the declaration of
[tool-e33] read_file ->    530	         [num_domains_orphaning_finalisers] variable in major_gc.c */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    531	      caml_finish_major_cycle(0);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    532	    }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    533	    CAMLassert(caml_gc_phase == Phase_sweep_and_mark_main);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    534	    CAMLassert (!f->updated_first);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    535	    CAMLassert (!f->updated_last);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    536	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    537	    /* Add the finalisers to [orph_structs] */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    538	    caml_plat_lock_blocking(&orphaned_lock);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    539	    f->next = orph_structs.final_info;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    540	    orph_structs.final_info = f;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    541	    caml_plat_unlock(&orphaned_lock);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    542	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    543	    /* Create a dummy final info */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    544	    f = domain_state->final_info = caml_alloc_final_info();
[tool-e33] read_file ->    545	    (void)caml_atomic_counter_decr(&num_domains_orphaning_finalisers);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    546	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    547	
[tool-e33] read_file ->    548	  /* [caml_orphan_finalisers] is called in a while loop in
[tool-e33] read_file ->    549	     [caml_domain_terminate].
[tool-e33] read_file ->    550	     We take care to decrement the [num_domains_to_final_update*] counters only
[tool-e33] read_file ->    551	     if we have not already decremented it for the current cycle. */
[tool-e33] read_file ->    552	  if(!f->updated_first) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    553	    (void)caml_atomic_counter_decr(&num_domains_to_final_update_first);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    554	    f->updated_first = 1;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    555	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    556	  if(!f->updated_last) {
[tool-e33] read_file ->    557	    (void)caml_atomic_counter_decr(&num_domains_to_final_update_last);
[tool-e33] read_file ->    558	    f->updated_last = 1;
[tool-e33] read_file ->    559	  }
[tool-e33] read_file ->    560	}
